Let me understand the task. We have a System F with:
- Locally nameless representation? Actually it's a mix: type variables are `Ty_BVar` (de Bruijn) and `Ty_FVar` (free names) with opening operations. Terms have `tm_bvar` and `tm_fvar`, with opening of terms via `open_tm`.

Interesting: `open_tm t u` substitutes u for the index-0 bvar in t. Note that `tm_abs` doesn't increment the binder index in `open_tm_rec`? Let's look:

```coq
Fixpoint open_tm_rec (k : nat) (u t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => if Nat.eqb k i then u else tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (open_tm_rec (S k) u t1)
  ...
```

Yes, tm_abs increments k. But tm_tabs does NOT increment k (correct, since it's a type binder). And tm_ty (type opening) increments in Ty_All and tm_tabs.

The typing rules:
- T_Abs and T_TAbs are in "locally nameless with names" style: they open the body with a fresh free variable, and use cofinite quantification with (L : list atom).
- T_TAbs: has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tabs t) (Ty_All T) if for fresh X: has_type (X :: Delta) Gamma (open_tm_ty t (Ty_FVar X)) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)).
- T_TApp: has_type Delta Gamma t (Ty_All T), wf_ty Delta U => has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tapp t U) (open_ty T U).

Values are only abs and tabs, requiring locally_closed_tm. Note that `value v` in the target theorem. Step: ST_AppAbs requires locally_closed_tm (tm_abs T t) and value v, then tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> open_tm t v. Note open_tm t v substitutes v for the outermost bvar of t. Interesting: the term is opened once; no closing operation is used in the semantics, so terms can have bvars referring to their argument.

Hmm, wait: `ST_AppAbs`: `tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> open_tm t v`. Since the term representation uses de Bruijn for term variables and the abstractions are "pre-opened" with free variables in the typing rule... there's a mismatch: T_Abs types the term `open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x)`, but the step reduces `tm_abs T t` applied to v by `open_tm t v`. That's the standard "open with arbitrary term" semantics for locally nameless; fine.

So we need to prove: for all t with has_type [] empty t (∀X. X → X), for all closed U (wf_ty [] U), value v, has_type [] empty v U, we have tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v.

This is a parametricity free theorem. Since t : ∀X. X → X and everything is closed, t must be either a type abstraction whose body returns its argument, or a nondeterministic choice among such. So the natural proof: logical relation.

Plan: Define a logical relation over types indexed by well-formedness of the type in the empty type context and closedness of the term:

Define `rel : ty -> tm -> Prop` such that
- rel (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t := forall u, value u -> rel T1 u -> (exists w, tm_app t u -->* w /\ rel T2 w) — need to be careful: the target conclusion is `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v` where `v` is a value. So for the identity function type ∀X. X→X, the relation should say: for all U (wf), for all value v : U, tm_app (t U) v -->* v.

Actually the canonical approach: define a "logical relation" as a function `ty -> tm -> Prop` defined by recursion on the type's structure, but note types here are locally nameless with `Ty_All` bodies containing bvars. To interpret `Ty_All T` we'd need to substitute/instantiate with a type U and interpret the result. The standard approach for locally nameless: interpret `Ty_All T` as: for all X fresh, for all U wf, the relation at `open_ty T U`.

But `Ty_All`'s interpretation depends on relation at `open_ty T U` for arbitrary U. Since `rel` depends on types recursively, we need well-founded recursion on the structure of the type. `open_ty T U` for a Ty_All-body: the body is a type; the relation at open_ty T U must be defined. Since open_ty T U may be larger? It's a substitution instance of T's body. We can define rel by recursion on type structure: rel (Ty_All T) t := forall (U : ty), wf_ty [] U -> rel (open_ty T U) t' ... but then rel is not structurally recursive on open_ty T U — the recursion is on T, and open_ty T U has the same structure as T except bvar 0 replaced by U. Hmm, but to define rel by Fixpoint on ty, we need rel (open_ty T U) where open_ty T U is not a subterm of Ty_All T.

Standard trick: define rel by recursion on the "size" of the type or use a two-level interpretation: interpret types with a type substitution into a semantic domain of "relations", i.e. define the interpretation of a type with a type environment.

Alternative approach: define rel T t by recursion where the Ty_All case is defined for a type U... but since we only need the particular type `∀X. X → X`, we might avoid a fully general logical relation and instead do a direct argument.

Let me think about the direct approach. t : ∀X. X → X with has_type [] empty t. What can t be? Typing rules: has_type [] empty t (Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar 0))). The only rule concluding Ty_All is T_TAbs. So U is closed with no bvars: corresponds to lc_ty_at 0 U. Probably we can prove lc_ty_at 0 U from wf_ty [] U, or at least lc_ty_at n U for any n >= 0. Actually lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_BVar 0) is false. Since wf_ty [] has no bvar rule at all, no bvars can appear. So lc_ty_at k U for any k. Might need a lemma: wf_ty Delta U -> lc_ty_at k U (for any k), plus free var condition? lc_ty_at doesn't check free vars, so yes, all wf types are locally closed at any k (need induction; WF_All gives lc_ty_at (S k) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) ... hmm, actually the WF_All case gives a body T with forall X fresh, wf_ty (X::Delta) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)). To conclude lc_ty_at k (Ty_All T), need lc_ty_at (S k) T, not open_ty. That's a problem: we need to relate lc_ty_at of T with open_ty T (Ty_FVar X). It's probably provable: lc_ty_at (S k) T iff lc_ty_at k (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X))... Hmm, need a lemma about open_ty and lc_ty_at. Let me think about whether we even need wf_ty -> lc_ty_at.

We need locally_closed_ty U to apply ST_TAppTabs. Alternatively we can avoid using that lemma by using a different stepping strategy: `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v`. t = tm_tabs t0. The step relation requires locally_closed_ty U for ST_TAppTabs. In the target theorem, we have wf_ty [] U. So we need wf_ty [] U -> locally_closed_ty U (or something that lets the step go through).

Hmm, but maybe another approach: we need to show multi-step reduction to v. The reduction of `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v`:
- If t = tm_tabs t0: ST_TAppTabs reduces to open_tm_ty t0 U (needs locally_closed_tm (tm_tabs t0) and locally_closed_ty U), then ST_App1 applies with the next step? To apply ST_App1 to `tm_app (open_tm_ty t0 U) v`, we need locally_closed_tm v — we have value v which gives locally_closed_tm for abs/tabs. Good. Then we need to show open_tm_ty t0 U evaluates appropriately...

But t might also be a tm_choice, since T_Choice can have any type. So t could be `tm_choice t1 t2` where each ti : ∀X. X→X. Or t could be `tm_fvar x` if there's a binding, but empty context means no. Actually has_type [] empty (tm_fvar x) T requires lookup_context x empty = Some T, impossible. So closed t of that type is either tm_tabs or tm_choice (nested). But careful: t may also be tm_app, tm_tapp, etc. But typing constrains it: T_App gives type T2 which is not of form Ty_All? T2 could be Ty_All! So a general logical relation / fundamental theorem seems needed. Let's think about how to set it up.

Approach: Prove a semantic soundness (fundamental lemma) using a logical relation over types, defined for all locally well-formed types with respect to a type environment. Since types use locally nameless representation with bvars and fvars, a standard route is:

Let's think about the structure of the problem differently. Maybe full generality isn't needed. We need only the free theorem for type ∀X. X → X. The fundamental theorem would need to handle all types in derivation, but perhaps we can prove a targeted lemma.

Let me consider proving a general "identity-like" semantic result via a logical predicate that only talks about the specific shape X → X after substituting.

Approach: Define a family of relations indexed by a type environment for fvars, where each fvar is assigned a "semantic type" = a pair of a type and a relation. Then `R_sigma T t` is defined by recursion on T:
- Ty_BVar i: shouldn't occur (or we can treat via sigma with de Bruijn levels?).
- Ty_FVar X: use sigma X (relation).
- Ty_Arrow T1 T2: t is a term such that for all value u with R T1 u, exists w, tm_app t u -->* w /\ R T2 w (plus maybe value w).
- Ty_All T: t is a term such that for all fresh X (not free in T? or in sigma) and all closed types V with wf, and all interpretations... t[V] behaves as R_{sigma[X:=V]} T.

The problem: R for the body needs a de Bruijn/substitution handling. We can define R by recursion on the type where Ty_All case is: for all X fresh and all V, R_{sigma[X := V]} (Ty_All's body?) — no, open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)... Hmm, but recursion on T: the Ty_All case requires R at type open_ty T (Ty_FVar X) which is not a subterm. But if we define R via a function `interp` on types with an explicit parameter for binders (like de Bruijn levels), we can avoid substitution: interp takes a *list* of semantic types for the currently bound type variables (de Bruijn levels), and sigma for the free ones:
- Ty_BVar i => nth i levels (should be a "semantic type" containing a type U and a relation R_U on terms along with wf).
- Ty_FVar X => sigma X (also a semantic type)
- Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => arrow relation
- Ty_All T => for all semantic type s (with type U_s wf): interp (s :: levels) sigma T.

But careful: the locally nameless convention with bvar numbering: in `open_ty T U`, bvar 0 is replaced by U throughout T (with binder increments in Ty_All). So in `Ty_All T`, the body T's bvar 0 refers to the binder, bvar 1 refers to the enclosing binder, etc. So a levels list indexed by bvar works: `interp levels sigma T`, where Ty_BVar i = nth i levels, and Ty_All T' => forall s, interp (s::levels) sigma T'. And Ty_FVar X => sigma X. Then indeed `interp levels sigma (open_ty T U) = interp (sem U :: levels) sigma T` — this requires a lemma, but the important thing is the definition is structurally recursive on T, so it terminates regardless.

Actually we don't even need the opening lemma if the typing rules can be expressed directly with the levels-list interpretation. Let's see: the typing rule T_TAbs: for all X fresh, has_type (X :: Delta) Gamma (open_tm_ty t (Ty_FVar X)) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)). To use the inductive hypothesis we need to interpret the opened type `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` at levels `sem(X) :: levels` — but sigma maps X to its semantic type, so `interp levels sigma (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X))` should equal `interp (sigma X :: levels) sigma T`. So we need the opening lemma anyway (or we can define interpretation of open types via the semantic analogue).

Standard approach: Define `sem_ty` as a record with type U and relation R, and prove lemmas:
1. `interp_open : interp levels sigma (open_ty T U) <-> interp (sem U :: levels) sigma T` — where `sem U` is the "identity" semantic type for U? Actually in the Ty_All case, quantification over all semantic types s (with underlying type U_s and relation R_s) — but where do these semantic types come from? Hmm, the theorem requires: for value v with has_type [] empty v U, we get tm_app (t U) v -->* v. So the relation for U should be "R_U v := exists? ..." Let's think: we want, ultimately, to show tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v. If we set the relation at U to be `fun v => has_type [] empty v U /\ value v /\ ...`, and the LR says `R (∀X.X→X) t` gives for all semantic type s: R (X→X) (tapp t U_s), and R (X→X) at U with relation R_U: for all value v with R_U v, tm_app (tapp t U) v -->* w with R_U w. If R_U is just "value + has_type at U", then w is a value of type U, not necessarily v itself. That's not enough to conclude w = v!

Hmm right, the free theorem says the result is *exactly* v. For nondeterministic choice, we must show there is a reduction path where the function returns the argument. A logical relation of the usual "weak" kind doesn't give equality of results.

Let's think about the semantic interpretation needed: for type ∀X.X→X, the relation should be something like: for all U (with semantic relation R), for all values v with R v, tm_app (t U) v -->* v — actually with the stronger requirement that the result is literally v. Can we prove such a strong relation by induction on typing derivations? Let's think about what can be typed at type ∀X.X→X:

- T_TAbs: t = tm_tabs t0, and for fresh X, has_type [X] empty (open_tm_ty t0 (fvar X)) (X → X). Now t0's opened body has type X→X with X a free type variable. The relation for X in the environment is some R at type U.

The body of type X→X: by T_Abs, it's λ:Y. body with Y a term variable (opened with fvar x), and body of type X. Terms of type X with environment x:X where X is a type variable can be: tm_fvar x (from T_Var), or tm_choice of such terms, or tm_app/tm_tapp with result type X... Can T_App have result type X? T_App concludes T2; T2 = X possible: t1 : T1 → X, t2 : T1. But can anything have type T1 → X where X is an fvar? T_Abs gives Arrow. t1 could be λ:... but has_type needs wf_ty [X] (T1 → X); wf_ty requires free fvars in Delta=[X]. So T1 can be X, or pointer... T1 could be X itself. Then t1 : X → X and t2 : X, so t1 t2 : X. But t1 could be the variable x : X → X? x has type X (the term var x : X). Hmm, x : X, not X→X.

Wait, the context Gamma has x : X where X is the type variable. So terms of type X in context x:X: tm_fvar x, tm_choice thereof, and also tm_app (tm_tapp ...)... Consider t1 : X→X — could it be an application of something? In a context with only x : X, can we type anything at type X→X? T_TAbs: no (needs Ty_All type). T_Abs: λ:(X→X)... wait T_Abs concludes (T1 → T2); to conclude X → X we need T1 = X and T2 = X; body must have type X in context x:X, y:X. Body could be y (the new var), giving λy. y : X → X. So yes, t1 could be λy.y, and then t1 t2 : X where t2 : X, e.g., x. So the identity function on X is typable in context x:X.

So the term of type X → X in context x:X can be complicated, but it still computes the identity on X-values? In System F with only these constructs (no fixpoints, no base types), every closed term of type ∀X. X→X is indeed the identity in a suitable sense — the classic "free theorem". The proof requires a logical relation where the interpretation of X is a "value relation" satisfying some condition (identity-like). Hmm.

Key: the classic proof uses the "identity extension lemma" or more simply: interpret X with the relation "term equality"... Let's recall how such free theorems are usually proven for System F: the logical relation R_T relates terms of type T; for type variable X, R_X is an arbitrary relation. Then the fundamental theorem gives: t ∈ R_{∀X.X→X}, meaning for any relation R ⊆ V×V, tapp t U ∈ R_{X→X} for both X-interpretations... Standard identity extension: with R_X := equality on terms of type X, we get that t app v = v. But in our setting, we need the relation to be on terms in the *untyped* language, where the type U is fixed. For a free theorem of the form "t U v reduces to v", we need a "parametricity" argument that gives equality (or reduction to v), not just relatedness.

Let me think about the standard proof of the free theorem "any f : ∀X. X→X is the identity" in a total language: take any closed type U and value v:U. To show f U v = v, use relational parametricity with the relation R relating (v, v) only? Hmm, that gives f U v R f U v (with the same U on both sides). To show it equals v we'd need to set up a relation R between U and another type V where the relation distinguishes... Classic trick: use the logical relation with free type variable X instantiated by two different types and a relation between them. Specifically, to prove f U v = v, consider the relation R ⊆ U × U given by graph? Hmm.

Alternatively, in our setting with reduction, we might just prove directly by "syntactic" analysis: induction on typing derivations, using the structure of the language. Since the language is strongly normalizing? Actually with choice, still strongly normalizing (no fixpoint). We want a *specific reduction path* to v (the left branch choices). We can prove by induction on typing derivation that certain terms "return their argument" along some path, but the complication is that a term of type X in context x:X may reduce to x or to choice... hmm, but eventually to the argument.

Maybe a syntactic normalization argument combined with a logical relation over types indexed by term-level behaviors would be simplest if we define the relation carefully to capture the exact behavior "applied to v reduces to v".

Let me consider defining the LR with the specific goal. We need to handle:
- Type variables: relation should be "the term reduces to ... "? Actually here's a thought: interpret type variable X with the relation `R_X := fun t => t -->* v` for the specific v we care about... no wait, we need it for all values.

Let me look at the structure of the theorem again:

forall t, has_type [] empty t (∀X. X→X) -> forall U v, wf_ty [] U -> value v -> has_type [] empty v U -> tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v.

Since the term is closed, the proof should be a parametricity argument. The standard "free theorem" proof for this uses a logical relation that is defined on *closed* terms and the identity extension lemma:

Step 1: define the LR where for type variable X, the relation is "the graph of the identity" on the chosen type U (i.e., relate each value to itself). Then prove fundamental theorem: for any T with no free type variables... hmm, but the free theorem needs *two* instantiations to get the equality with v. Let me recall the classic proof:

The free theorem for f : ∀X. X→X: for any types A, B, any relation R ⊆ A×B, if (a, b) ∈ R then (f A a, f B b) ∈ R. Taking A = B = U, R = {(v, v)}: (v,v) ∈ R, so (f U v, f U v) ∈ R, meaning f U v = v. Yes! The relation R is a singleton set {(v,v)}, not equality on all of U. So the relation at X is chosen to be exactly the singleton relation on the specific value pair.

In our setting, we have only one type U and terms are untyped, so we can take the relation at X to be {(v, v)} — using the *same* type U for both sides? Hmm, the classic argument uses two different types A and B for the two instantiations, but here we have a single type U and the term t U is a single term. Hmm, but f A a and f B b are two different instantiations of the same polymorphic term; here we can't even type-instantiate t at two different types and compare because the language has no type-level equality... Actually we can: t U and t U are the same term. But to get f U v = v we need the relation argument. Let's think.

The free theorem's parametricity proof for a closed polymorphic term t: ∀X. X→X gives, for any two types A, B and relation R ⊆ A×B: (f A, f B) ∈ ⟦X→X⟧(R), i.e., for any (a, b) ∈ R, (f A a, f B b) ∈ R. Since the term is the same t, f A = tapp t A and f B = tapp t B. Now if we could take A = U, B = U with the same term... but the same term tapp t U appears on both sides — that's fine: (tapp t U, tapp t U) ∈ ⟦...⟧ means: for all (a,b) ∈ R, (tapp t U a, tapp t U b) ∈ R. Take R = {(v,v)}: then (tapp t U v, tapp t U v) ∈ R = {(v,v)}, so tapp t U v = v! 

But in our setting, "=" is not what we get; we get reduction. So define the relation semantically as: pairs (t1, t2) such that t1 -->* w and t2 -->* w for some common w? Or better: define ⟦X⟧ = {(a, b) | a -->* v0 and b -->* v0} for a fixed value v0 (the v from the theorem). Then the arrow relation: (a, b) ∈ ⟦X→X⟧ iff for all (a', b') ∈ ⟦X⟧, (app a a', app b b') ∈ ⟦X⟧. And ⟦∀X. body⟧ needs the relation to be "valid"/"admissible" and quantification over all relations... Hmm, but we need the relation at X to be an arbitrary relation and the interpretation of ∀X to quantify over relations. In our simply untyped setting, relations can be arbitrary relations on terms. Then (tapp t U, tapp t U) ∈ ⟦∀X.X→X⟧ means for all relations R on terms, (tapp t U, tapp t U) ∈ ⟦X→X⟧(R). Hmm, but that's the interpretation where both sides use the same type U. What exactly is the interpretation of a type? A pair of terms (t1, t2) ∈ ⟦T⟧ where both terms are "of type T" in the semantic sense, with the type environment being a relation R (for the free variable X). But here's the thing: the term on the left and right are both instantiated at U... For the free theorem to give us something, we need the relation R at X to be nonempty and instantiate both sides with the same term. So define the LR as a binary relation on terms, indexed by a type and an environment mapping type variables to binary relations on terms; and for the ∀ case, quantify over all binary relations R:

(t1, t2) ∈ ⟦∀X.T⟧ iff for all binary relations R on tm, (t1, t2) ∈ ⟦T⟧(R :: env)? Wait, but the type tapp: (tapp t U, tapp t U) should be in ⟦X→X⟧(R). But the types: the body of ∀ is a type T with bvar 0; ⟦T⟧ needs the instantiation type for bvar 0; in a two-type setting, the two sides can be instantiated at different types, but here both at U. Hmm, in the standard setup, the LR for System F is a ternary relation: type T, term t1, term t2, where t1 has type T[types for left] and t2 has type T[right]. Here we can use both sides at U. Now, the main logical relation. Let me reconsider the approach with the translation of type fvars. Actually, maybe there's an even simpler path given the specific theorem. Let me think again about whether a full fundamental theorem is needed or whether we can prove a specialized semantic lemma.

The challenge: t : ∀X.X→X is a *closed* term but its typing derivation uses T_TAbs, T_Abs, T_Var, T_App, T_TApp, T_Choice. To prove the free theorem, we can prove a "logical relation" statement by induction over typing derivations. Since the type is fixed as ∀X.X→X, but subderivations have other types (X→X, X, etc.), we need the relation at those types. The relation at X is a free parameter (a predicate R). So a general fundamental theorem over all types T is the clean way.

I think the instantiation approach is standard and workable. Let's define:

`ty_inst (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty` — replace free fvars by sigma.
`tm_inst (sigma : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm` — replace free fvars in type annotations.
and `tm_subst_env (rho : atom -> tm) t` — replace free term fvars.
Combined `inst rho sigma t := tm_subst_env rho (tm_inst sigma t)`.

Hmm, we need commutation lemmas:
- open_tm (tm_subst_env rho t) u = tm_subst_env (rho[x:=u]) (open_tm t (tm_fvar x)) when x fresh (not free in t, and not in rho's domain? Let's think).
- open_tm_ty (tm_inst sigma t) U = tm_inst (sigma[X:=U]) (open_tm_ty t (Ty_FVar X)) when X fresh.

These should be provable by induction on t with appropriate side conditions. The side conditions: x not free in t (syntactically, as an fvar occurrence?) and X not free in t's annotations. We get freshness from the cofinite premise: we can choose x not in L; but L only contains... the rule's L is arbitrary; the premise holds for all x ∉ L. We need x fresh w.r.t. t, Gamma, rho. We can choose such x by taking a fresh atom: since the premise holds for all x not in L, and there are infinitely many atoms, we can pick x ∉ L ∪ (free vars of t) ∪ (domain considerations). Since the premise is universally quantified over x ∉ L, we can instantiate at any fresh x. So define a fresh atom function or use a big list L' = L ++ all relevant vars and pick x fresh w.r.t. L' via a lemma `exists_fresh : forall L, exists x, ~ In x L`. We can define `fresh L := fold max L + 1` or use a simple construction. Since atoms are nat, fresh L := S (max of L) works.

Now, V definitions:

```coq
Fixpoint V (binds : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => st_rel (nth_default binds i) t
  | Ty_FVar X => st_rel (sigma X) t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => value t /\ forall u, V binds sigma T1 u -> C binds sigma T2 (tm_app t u)
  | Ty_All T1 => value t /\ forall (U : ty) (Hwf : wf_ty [] U) (R : tm -> Prop)
                        (HR : forall w, R w -> value w),
                   C (mk_sem U Hwf R HR :: binds) sigma T1 (tm_tapp t U)
  end
with C (binds : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed_tm t /\ exists v, value v /\ t -->* v /\ V binds sigma T v.
```

So the right way to think: the semantic interpretation V binds sigma T assumes T's free bvars are indexed relative to a context where the enclosing binders are the *pushed* list. This is the standard issue: the raw type ops are "non-shifting" and the interpretation needs adjustment.

Alternative standard approach: interpret types via *closing substitution*: instead of V on raw open types, define the semantics of T relative to an environment `delta : list sem_ty` where the list is in the same index order, and note the semantics of `open_ty T U` should use the env `sem U :: delta` but with U's type substituted. To make the induction go through, we might define a *shifted* interpretation: `V binds sigma T` where each Ty_BVar i looks up binds.(i) — and prove `V (s :: binds) sigma T = V binds sigma (shift T?)`. Let me write:

```coq
Record sem_ty := {
  sty_ty : ty;
  sty_wf : wf_ty [] sty_ty;
  sty_rel : tm -> Prop;
  sty_rel_value : forall t, sty_rel t -> value t
}.

Definition default_sem : sem_ty.
Proof.
  refine {| sty_ty := Ty_All (Ty_BVar 0); sty_rel := fun _ => False |}.
  - apply WF_All with (L := []). intros X _. cbn. apply WF_Var. now left.
  - intros t H. destruct H.
Defined.
```

Now the relations V and C:

```coq
Fixpoint V (binds : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => sty_rel (nth_default i binds) t
  | Ty_FVar X => sty_rel (sigma X) t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => value t /\ (forall u, V binds sigma T1 u -> C binds sigma T2 (tm_app t u))
  | Ty_All T1 => value t /\ (forall (U : ty) (Hwf : wf_ty [] U) (R : tm -> Prop) (HR : forall w, R w -> value w),
                                C (mk_sem U Hwf R HR :: binds) sigma T1 (tm_tapp t U))
  end
with C (binds : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  locally_closed_tm t /\ (exists v, value v /\ t -->* v /\ V binds sigma T v).
```

Now the fundamental theorem. Let me define the semantic environments and instantiation.

Instantiation of types by sigma:
```coq
Fixpoint ty_inst (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sigma X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_inst sigma T1) (ty_inst sigma T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_inst sigma T1)
  end.
```
Note: substituting fvars; bvars unchanged (they're bound or refer to external binders—but we only instantiate closed types, so no free bvars).

Instantiation of terms:
```coq
Fixpoint tm_ty_inst (sigma : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_inst sigma T) (tm_ty_inst sigma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_ty_inst sigma t1) (tm_ty_inst sigma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_ty_inst sigma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_ty_inst sigma t1) (ty_inst sigma T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_ty_inst sigma t1) (tm_ty_inst sigma t2)
  end.
```

Term substitution environment:
```coq
Fixpoint tm_inst (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => rho x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (tm_inst rho t1)
  ...
```

Combined: `inst rho sigma t := tm_inst rho (tm_ty_inst sigma t)` or the other order; they commute (type substitution and term substitution affect different things).

Goal: t : ∀X.X→X closed. We can prove by induction on the typing derivation a *strong syntactic* property:

Let me think more about the proof strategy. "Binary type-indexed logical relation" suggests defining a relation `R T t1 t2` (binary on terms) indexed by types. This is the classic parametricity setup where the two sides can be different instantiations. But our theorem only has one term. Hmm, perhaps the intended relation is binary because the type is ∀X.X→X and the free theorem is proven by relating two instantiations (e.g., at U with relation R between U and U). The "binary" could be the standard two-term relation where the same term appears on both sides.

Let me think about how the binary version solves the annotation problem. With a binary relation, the semantic interpretation of ∀X.T at env ρ (binary relations) is: (t1, t2) ∈ ⟦∀X.T⟧ρ iff for all U1 U2 R, (t1 U1, t2 U2) ∈ ⟦T⟧(ρ, X↦(U1,U2,R)). For the final theorem, take t1 = t2 = t, U1 = U2 = U, and R = {(v,v)}. Then (t U, t U) ∈ ⟦X→X⟧(X↦{(v,v)}). Applying to (v,v): (t U v, t U v) ∈ R = {(v,v)}, so t U v = v syntactically. Then, since we need reduction not equality, define R as {w | w -->* v}? But with a binary relation, R = {(a,b) | a -->* v ∧ b -->* v} and membership of (t U v, t U v) gives t U v -->* v ✓.

But the T_TAbs case still has the annotation problem: to prove (tabs t0, tabs t0) ∈ ⟦∀X.T⟧, we need for all U1,U2,R: (tapp (tabs t0) U1, tapp (tabs t0) U2) ∈ ⟦T⟧(X↦(U1,U2,R)). After stepping, the terms are open_tm_ty t0 U1 and open_tm_ty t0 U2. The IH (from the typing derivation opened with fvar X) gives a relation between terms open_tm_ty t0 (fvar X) *on both sides*: (open_tm_ty t0 (fvar X), open_tm_ty t0 (fvar X)) ∈ ⟦open_ty T (fvar X)⟧(X↦(U1,U2,R)). Wait, but the IH would need to be instantiated with sigma X = the pair (U1,U2,R), and the term on both sides is the same opened-with-fvar term. Now the actual terms open_tm_ty t0 U1 and open_tm_ty t0 U2 differ from the IH's terms by annotations again! Hmm, U1 vs U2 vs fvar X. Same issue.

Unless the binary relation is defined on terms *modulo type annotations*, e.g., relating the terms based only on their structure. But the arbitrary R at type variables...

The relation R is arbitrary; the soundness proof needs to show (t1 u1, t1 u2) ∈ R, and it gets that from the arrow relation's *definition*: the arrow relation at T1→X is defined as: for all related (u1,u2), C X (t1 u1) (t1 u2) — where C includes "there exist common reducts w1,w2 with R w1 w2". Unless we strengthen the invariant: prove the fundamental theorem for a *class* of relations closed under ≈, and show the class is closed under the operations (arrow, ∀-quantification over the class). Is the class of ≈-closed relations closed under the arrow construction? If R1, R2 are ≈-closed, is the arrow relation ≈-closed? The arrow relation: t ∈ R_{T1→T2} iff value t ∧ forall u ∈ R1, C R2 (t u). For t ≈ t': value same; for u ∈ R1, need C R2 (t u) ↔ C R2 (t' u). C R2 x = lc x ∧ exists v, x -->* v ∧ R2 v. Since x = t u and x' = t' u are ≈-equivalent (if t ≈ t' and same u), and reduction correspondence gives reducts v, v' with v ≈ v'; then R2 v ↔ R2 v' if R2 is ≈-closed ✓. Now, the fundamental theorem with term instantiation but no type instantiation. Let's define:

`inst_tm (sigma : atom -> ty) (rho : atom -> tm) t := tm_subst_env rho (tm_ty_inst sigma t)`.

Wait, term substitution and type substitution commute? tm_subst replaces term fvars; tm_ty_inst replaces type fvars in annotations. They commute ✓ (they act on disjoint parts). Let's define inst as: replace the term's own type annotations according to sigma, and replace term fvars with rho x *without* further instantiation. This is a simultaneous substitution. Define:

```
Fixpoint inst (sigma : atom -> ty) (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => rho x
  | tm_abs T b => tm_abs (ty_inst sigma T) (inst sigma rho b)
  ...
```
This is fine.

Now the env_ok: for each x with Gamma(x) = Some T, V [] sigma T (rho x) — wait, but the *type* T has fvars from Delta; we interpret it via sigma in V, so no instantiation needed. And rho x is a term (should be a value with V T). ✓.

Then inst: rho' x = u, and other fvars via rho, annotations via sigma. On the other hand, open_tm (inst sigma rho t2) u: opens the outermost free var of the instantiated body with u. Are these equal? Let me now also handle the substitution commutation for `inst` and openings:

Define:
```
Definition sty_of (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) : atom -> ty := fun X => sty_ty (sigma X).
Fixpoint ty_inst (s : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty := ...

Definition inst sigma rho t := tm_inst_env rho (tm_inst_ty (sty_of sigma) t).
```
Or simultaneous. Commutation: tm_inst_env_ty and tm_inst_ty commute. Let's do sequential: first type-instantiate the term, then term-substitute. But for the opening commutation, we need care: 
`inst sigma rho (open_tm t (tm_fvar x))` = tm_inst_env rho (tm_inst_ty sty (open_tm t (fvar x))).
We want = open_tm (tm_inst_env rho (tm_inst_ty sty t)) (rho x).
Step 1: tm_inst_ty sty (open_tm t (fvar x)) = open_tm (tm_inst_ty sty t) (tm_fvar x) (type instantiation commutes with term opening; the term opening replaces bvars with fvar x, unaffected by type annotation instantiation; but annotations *inside* t are type-instantiated; the annotation of any abs inside is instantiated; the opened fvar x is a term fvar; so this should hold ✓).
Step 2: tm_inst_env rho (open_tm t' (tm_fvar x)) = open_tm (tm_inst_env rho t') (rho x) (as discussed, holds unconditionally? Let's re-derive: open_tm t' (tm_fvar x) replaces the outermost free term bvar of t' with tm_fvar x. Then tm_inst_env rho replaces all free term fvars, including the introduced x and pre-existing ones. On the RHS, tm_inst_env rho t' replaces all free fvars; then open_tm with rho x replaces the outermost free bvar. Since rho doesn't affect bvars, the positions match; and the introduced x on the LHS becomes rho x on both sides ✓. Pre-existing fvar x's become rho x on both ✓. So unconditional equality ✓.)

Hmm wait, one subtlety: open_tm with a term u substitutes u at bvar positions; if u contains fvars, do they get substituted by rho on the RHS? On the RHS, we open *after* substitution with rho x — so rho x is inserted and its fvars are not further substituted ✓. On the LHS, we open with tm_fvar x first, then substitute rho: the inserted x → rho x, and if rho x contains fvars, they are NOT further substituted (substitution is simultaneous, not iterated) ✓. Good.

Now the V/C definitions need `nth_default` and the default sem_ty. Let me define:

```
Definition default_sem : sem_ty.
```
with sty_ty := Ty_All (Ty_BVar 0), sty_wf := (proof), sty_rel := fun _ => False, sty_rel_value := False_rect.

Then `nth_sem (E : list sem_ty) (i : nat) : sem_ty := match nth_error E i with Some s => s | None => default_sem end`.

Now, let's write V and C. Note that V's Ty_All case quantifies over `(U : ty) (Hwf : wf_ty [] U) (R : tm -> Prop) (HR : forall w, R w -> value w)`. The resulting sem_ty is `mk_sem U Hwf R HR`.

```
Fixpoint V (binds : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => sty_rel (nth_sem binds i) t
  | Ty_FVar X => sty_rel (sigma X) t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => value t /\ forall u, V binds sigma T1 u -> C binds sigma T2 (tm_app t u)
  | Ty_All T1 => value t /\ forall U Hwf R HR,
      C (mk_sem U Hwf R HR :: binds) sigma T1 (tm_tapp t U)
  end
with C (binds : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  locally_closed_tm t /\ exists v, t -->* v /\ V binds sigma T v
```

Note: value t in C's witness is implied by V ✓ (prove V_value). Let me include it or not; V_value gives it.

Hmm, one concern: in the Ty_All case of V, `C (mk_sem ... :: binds) sigma T1 (tm_tapp t U)` — C recursively calls V on T1, which is structurally smaller ✓. Good, the Fixpoint is structurally recursive on T (both V and C recurse on the same T or subterms). Coq should accept the mutual fixpoint.

Now, the "V_value" lemma: V binds sigma T t -> value t. Induction on T:
- BVar/FVar: sty_rel_value ✓.
- Arrow/All: conjunction first component ✓.
This requires the mutual induction? V_value doesn't need C. But V is defined mutually with C, so the induction principle for V might be awkward. Since V and C are defined by a mutual Fixpoint, we get `V_ind`/`V_rect` maybe with the mutual scheme. Alternatively, define C *after* V as a Definition (not Fixpoint), because C's recursion into V is not structural on a type that needs mutual recursion? Let's see: C binds sigma T t := lc t ∧ exists v, t -->* v ∧ V binds sigma T v. This is a plain definition using V, no recursion needed! Then V's Ty_All case uses C at T1 — but C is defined in terms of V, and V's definition mentions C, so it's a circular dependency. We can define V first if C were inlined: in the Ty_All case, write `locally_closed_tm (tm_tapp t U) ∧ exists v, ... ∧ V (mk_sem...::binds) sigma T1 v`. That's self-referential through V at T1 (structurally smaller) — but C is inlined, so V's definition contains a recursive call to V at T1 inside the exists. That's fine structurally. So define:

```
Fixpoint V binds sigma T t {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => sty_rel (nth_sem binds i) t
  | Ty_FVar X => sty_rel (sigma X) t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => value t /\ forall u, V binds sigma T1 u ->
        (locally_closed_tm (tm_app t u) /\ exists v, tm_app t u -->* v /\ V binds sigma T2 v)
  | Ty_All T1 => value t /\ forall U Hwf R HR,
        locally_closed_tm (tm_tapp t U) /\
        exists v, tm_tapp t U -->* v /\ V (mk_sem U Hwf R HR :: binds) sigma T1 v
  end.
Definition C binds sigma T t := locally_closed_tm t /\ exists v, t -->* v /\ V binds sigma T v.
```
This is a clean structurally-recursive Fixpoint ✓. Then C is just a definition. And lemmas about C unfold easily.

But hmm, V's arrow case: should the result be C T2 (tm_app t u) with lc of the *application*: `locally_closed_tm (tm_app t u)` is automatic from lc t (value) and lc u (value); we could omit it. But for uniformity, keep it as C. We can prove lc of tm_app from lc of components via a lemma. Actually including it means when proving V at arrow for a value t, we must prove lc (tm_app t u) — easy from value t and value u.

Let me simplify V's arrow case to: `value t /\ forall u, V binds sigma T1 u -> C binds sigma T2 (tm_app t u)` with C defined *after*? Circular. To avoid circularity, inline C's definition as above, or make C a Fixpoint mutually. Inlining is fine.

Actually, let's define V with inlined C-expansions and then C as a Definition. Let me write:

```
Fixpoint V (binds : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => sty_rel (nth_sem binds i) t
  | Ty_FVar X => sty_rel (sigma X) t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value t /\
      forall u, V binds sigma T1 u ->
        locally_closed_tm (tm_app t u) /\ exists v, tm_app t u -->* v /\ V binds sigma T2 v
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      value t /\
      forall (U : ty) (Hwf : wf_ty [] U) (R : tm -> Prop) (HR : forall w, R w -> value w),
        locally_closed_tm (tm_tapp t U) /\ exists v, tm_tapp t U -->* v /\ V (mk_sem U Hwf R HR :: binds) sigma T1 v
  end.

Definition C binds sigma T t : Prop :=
  locally_closed_tm t /\ exists v, t -->* v /\ V binds sigma T v.
```

OK. Now let me think about whether the arrow case's `locally_closed_tm (tm_app t u)` is provable when t is a value and u is a value: lc_tm_at 0 0 (tm_app t u) needs lc_tm_at 0 0 t and u ✓ (value gives lc).

And V_value: V binds sigma T t -> value t ✓.

Now, the opening lemma for V with binds. Let me write the statement precisely:

```
Lemma V_open_general : forall k (E : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T V0 : ty) (s : sem_ty),
  length E = k ->
  lc_ty_at (S k) T ->
  (forall (E' : list sem_ty) t, V E' sigma V0 t <-> sty_rel s t) ->
  forall t, V E sigma (open_ty_rec k V0 T) t <-> V (E ++ [s]) sigma T t.
```

Hmm, the hypothesis `forall E' t, V E' sigma V0 t <-> sty_rel s t` is strong: it says V at V0 with any env equals s's relation. For V0 = fvar X and s = sigma X: V E' sigma (fvar X) = sty_rel (sigma X) ✓ trivially, no env dependence. For V0 = U lc at 0 and s = mk_sem U (V [] sigma U): V E' sigma U ↔ V [] sigma U by env-independence; and V [] sigma U = sty_rel (mk_sem U (V [] sigma U)) ✓ by definition (the record field sty_rel is exactly V [] sigma U). ✓.

Now the induction proof of V_open_general. Let's detail it:

T = Ty_BVar i:
  lc_ty_at (S k) (BVar i) → i < S k → i ≤ k.
  Goal: V E sigma (open_ty_rec k V0 (BVar i)) t ↔ V (E ++ [s]) sigma (BVar i) t.
  Note open_ty_rec k V0 (BVar i) = if Nat.eqb k i then V0 else BVar i.
  Case i = k: LHS = V E sigma V0 t = sty_rel s t (hypothesis). RHS = sty_rel (nth_sem (E ++ [s]) k) t. Since length E = k, nth_sem (E ++ [s]) k = s ✓.
  Case i < k: LHS = sty_rel (nth_sem E i) t; RHS = sty_rel (nth_sem (E ++ [s]) i). Need nth_sem (E++[s]) i = nth_sem E i when i < length E ✓.

  But careful: `Nat.eqb k i` returns a bool; need to destruct and use eq/neq. Use `Nat.eqb_eq`, `Nat.eqb_neq`.

T = Ty_FVar X: both = sty_rel (sigma X) t ✓ (open_ty_rec doesn't change fvars).

T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2:
  open_ty_rec k V0 (Arrow T1 T2) = Arrow (open_ty_rec k V0 T1) (open_ty_rec k V0 T2).
  V E sigma (Arrow A B) t = value t ∧ forall u, V E sigma A u -> C E sigma B (t u).
  Goal: value t ∧ forall u, V E sigma A u -> C E sigma B (t u) ↔ value t ∧ forall u, V (E++[s]) sigma T1 u -> C (E++[s]) sigma T2 (t u).
  Use IHs: V E sigma A u ↔ V (E++[s]) sigma T1 u ✓. For C: C E sigma B y = lc y ∧ exists v, y -->* v ∧ V E sigma B v. The lc and multi parts are the same; the V part uses IH for T2 ✓. So C E sigma B y ↔ C (E++[s]) sigma T2 y ✓.
  Then the forall equivalence: yes.

T = Ty_All T1:
  open_ty_rec k V0 (All T1) = All (open_ty_rec (S k) V0 T1).
  V E sigma (All A) t = value t ∧ forall U Hwf R HR, C (mk U R :: E) sigma A (tapp t U).
  V (E++[s]) sigma (All T1) t = value t ∧ forall U Hwf R HR, C (mk U R :: E ++ [s]) sigma T1 (tapp t U).
  For each U,R: apply IH with k := S k, E := mk U R :: E, and hypothesis length (mk U R :: E) = S k ✓. The IH's hypothesis `forall E' t, V E' sigma V0 t ↔ sty_rel s t` — same ✓. Gives:
  V (mk U R :: E) sigma (open_ty_rec (S k) V0 T1) x ↔ V ((mk U R :: E) ++ [s]) sigma T1 x = V (mk U R :: (E ++ [s])) sigma T1 x ✓.
  Then C equivalence: C (mk U R :: E) sigma (open_ty_rec (S k) V0 T1) y ↔ C (mk U R :: E ++ [s]) sigma T1 y (lc and multi same, V by IH) ✓.
  So the foralls match ✓.

Great, the lemma is straightforward given the ingredients. Also need `lc_ty_at (S (S k)) T1` from lc_ty_at (S k) (All T1) ✓ (inversion of lc_ty_all: lc_ty_at k' (Ty_All T) -> lc_ty_at (S k') T — that's just the constructor's inverse; `inversion` gives it).

Note the hypothesis `lc_ty_at (S k) T` is needed to rule out out-of-range bvars. ✓.

Now `V_env_indep` / `V_prefix`:
```
Lemma V_prefix : forall K T, lc_ty_at K T ->
  forall pre A B sigma t, V (pre ++ A) sigma T t <-> V (pre ++ B) sigma T t.
```
Hmm, wait: is this true? Let's check the base case Ty_BVar i with lc_ty_at K (BVar i): i < K. The lookups use nth_sem (pre ++ A) i (at depth 0). Since i < K = length pre, the lookup uses pre's entries in both ✓. Under Ty_All, we push s: env becomes s :: (pre ++ A) = (s :: pre) ++ A; and T1 is lc at S K; IH needs length (s::pre) = S K ✓.
Arrow: IHs ✓.
FVar ✓.
So yes. But note: the statement has V at any depth; the "pre" length must equal K. Let me state with the length condition: `length pre = K`.

Hmm, but there's a subtlety: in the Arrow/All cases, V recurses on subterms at the *same* K (arrow) or K+1 (all). ✓.

So:
```
Lemma V_prefix : forall K T, lc_ty_at K T -> forall pre, length pre = K ->
  forall A B sigma t, V (pre ++ A) sigma T t <-> V (pre ++ B) sigma T t.
```

Then env-independence: `lc_ty_at 0 U -> V A sigma U t ↔ V B sigma U t` via pre = [] ✓.

Now, the sigma-agreement lemma:
```
Lemma V_sigma_agree : forall T sigma sigma', (forall X, In X (free_ty_vars T) -> sigma X = sigma' X) ->
  forall E t, V E sigma T t <-> V E sigma' T t.
```
Careful: under Ty_All, the body's free fvars are the same (fvars aren't bound); sigma-agreement for the body follows. But V's Ty_All case quantifies over sem_ty and pushes into binds, sigma unchanged ✓. The arrow: T1, T2 free fvars ⊆ free_ty_vars T ✓. The FVar case: fvar X is free in T → sigma X = sigma' X ✓. So the lemma is easy. But we need the free fvars of subterms to be subsets of free fvars T: `free_ty_vars (Arrow T1 T2) = free T1 ∪ free T2`; under All, `free_ty_vars (All T1) = free_ty_vars T1` ✓. Actually we can state it more simply: if sigma and sigma' agree on all atoms (pointwise), then V equal. But we need agreement on a subset. Let's define free_ty_vars via a Fixpoint and prove the subset relationships.

Alternatively, simpler: prove `V E sigma T t` depends on sigma only via the fvars in T by induction with the hypothesis agreeing on free_ty_vars T, using a lemma that free_ty_vars (subterm) ⊆ free_ty_vars T ✓.

Hmm, do we actually need this lemma? In the T_TAbs case, we have sigma_X = sigma[X ↦ s] and T's free fvars ⊆ Delta, X ∉ Delta. We need C [s] sigma_X T x ↔ C [s] sigma T x. The lc and multi parts don't depend on sigma; the V part does: V [s] sigma_X T x ↔ V [s] sigma T x. Since T's free fvars are in Delta and sigma_X agrees with sigma on Delta, and X is not free in T, the lemma gives it ✓.

We just need a "fresh atom not in a given list" lemma. Since atoms are nat, define `fresh (L : list atom) := fold_right max 0 L + 1` or S (max). Then ~ In (fresh L) L. Similarly for the T_Abs case: choose x fresh outside L and the atoms of t2 (term and type fvars). Then inst sigma rho' t2 = inst sigma rho t2 ✓ (rho' differs only at x, not free in t2).

How do we get "x ∉ atoms t2" and use it? We need a lemma: if x ∉ free term fvars of t2 then tm_inst_env rho' t2 = tm_inst_env rho t2 when rho and rho' agree elsewhere. Define free_tm_vars : tm -> list atom (term fvars, including annotations' type fvars? no, term fvars only). We can just use "atoms" lists that include everything (all fvars appearing anywhere), and prove the needed properties. Let's define:

```
Fixpoint atoms_ty (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => atoms_ty T1 ++ atoms_ty T2
  | Ty_All T1 => atoms_ty T1
  end.

Fixpoint atoms_tm (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs T b => atoms_ty T ++ atoms_tm b
  | tm_app t1 t2 => atoms_tm t1 ++ atoms_tm t2
  | tm_tabs b => atoms_tm b
  | tm_tapp b T => atoms_tm b ++ atoms_ty T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => atoms_tm t1 ++ atoms_tm t2
  end.
```

Then prove:
- `atoms_ty_subset`: for subterms? We can prove in the substitution lemmas directly.
- `tm_inst_env_ext`: if forall x, In x (atoms_tm t) -> rho x = rho' x, then tm_inst_env rho t = tm_inst_env rho' t.
- Similarly for type instantiation and atoms_ty.

Since we only substitute fvars, and atoms lists include all fvars, this works.

Let me now think about the proof of V_value (needed for HR in the splice lemma):
```
Lemma V_value : forall E sigma T t, V E sigma T t -> value t.
Proof. induction T; simpl; intros; try (destruct H; auto). 
- (* BVar *) apply sty_rel_value; auto.
- (* FVar *) apply sty_rel_value; auto.
Qed.
```
For BVar: V = sty_rel (nth_sem E n) t → sty_rel_value ✓.
For FVar: sty_rel (sigma X) ✓.
Arrow/All: destruct H as [Hv _]; exact Hv ✓.

Also `V_lc : V E sigma T t -> locally_closed_tm t` from V_value and value → lc ✓ (value's lc: v_abs gives locally_closed_tm, v_tabs gives locally_closed_tm).

Now, the opening lemma's hypothesis `forall E' t, V E' sigma V0 t <-> sty_rel s t`: for V0 = U (a type expression), s = mk_sem U (V [] sigma U) (with sty_lc, HR), this requires V E' sigma U t ↔ V [] sigma U t, which is V_prefix with K=0 and lc U ✓, and V [] sigma U t ↔ sty_rel s t = V [] sigma U t ✓.

For V0 = fvar X, s = sigma_X X: V E' sigma_X (fvar X) t = sty_rel (sigma_X X) t ✓ definitionally.

Now, the V_prefix lemma needs lc_ty_at K U. It's fine.

Let me now also think about the general lemma `V_open_general`'s use in T_TAbs: E = [], V0 = fvar X, s = sigma_X X. The hypothesis `forall E' t, V E' sigma_X (fvar X) t ↔ sty_rel (sigma_X X) t` holds definitionally ✓. The conclusion: V [] sigma_X (open_ty_rec 0 (fvar X) T) t ↔ V ([] ++ [sigma_X X]) sigma_X T t = V [s] sigma_X T t ✓.

And the C version: C [] sigma_X (open_ty T (fvar X)) x ↔ C [s] sigma_X T x — proven from the V version by unfolding C (lc and multi are env-independent) ✓.

Now let me also verify: does the statement `open_ty_rec 0 (fvar X) T` equal `open_ty T (fvar X)`? open_ty T U := open_ty_rec 0 U T ✓ (note argument order: open_ty T U = open_ty_rec 0 U T). In the typing rule it's `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` ✓.

Now let's think about `inst` and the sigma_X/substitution lemmas more concretely, and also the exact definitions.

Let me define:

```coq
Definition sty (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) : atom -> ty := fun X => sty_ty (sigma X).

Fixpoint ty_inst (s : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => s X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_inst s T1) (ty_inst s T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_inst s T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_inst (s : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T b => tm_abs (ty_inst s T) (tm_ty_inst s b)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_ty_inst s t1) (tm_ty_inst s t2)
  | tm_tabs b => tm_tabs (tm_ty_inst s b)
  | tm_tapp b T => tm_tapp (tm_ty_inst s b) (ty_inst s T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_ty_inst s t1) (tm_ty_inst s t2)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_rho_inst (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => rho x
  | tm_abs T b => tm_abs T (tm_rho_inst rho b)
  | ...
  end.

Definition inst (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  tm_rho_inst rho (tm_ty_inst (sty sigma) t).
```

Commutation lemmas:
1. `tm_ty_inst_open_tm`: tm_ty_inst s (open_tm_rec k u t) = open_tm_rec k (tm_ty_inst s u) (tm_ty_inst s t). Wait, open_tm_rec uses a *term* u to substitute; the type instantiation should apply to u too. In our use, u = tm_fvar x → tm_fvar x ✓. Let's state generally: tm_ty_inst s (open_tm t u) = open_tm (tm_ty_inst s t) (tm_ty_inst s u) ✓ (should hold).
2. `tm_rho_inst_open_tm`: tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm t u) = open_tm (tm_rho_inst rho t) (tm_rho_inst rho u)? Hmm, our earlier analysis said tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm t (tm_fvar x)) = open_tm (tm_rho_inst rho t) (rho x). More generally, tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm t u) = open_tm (tm_rho_inst rho t) (tm_rho_inst rho u) — is that true? Test t = tm_bvar 0, u = tm_bvar 1: LHS rho-inst = tm_bvar 0 (opened: t with bvar 0 replaced by u = tm_bvar 1 → tm_bvar 1) → tm_bvar 1. RHS: open_tm (tm_bvar 0) (tm_bvar 1) = tm_bvar 1 ✓. Test t = tm_app (tm_bvar 0) (tm_fvar x), u = tm_fvar y: LHS opened = tm_app (tm_fvar y) (tm_fvar x) → rho: tm_app (rho y) (rho x). RHS: rho-inst t = tm_app (tm_bvar 0) (rho x); open with rho y → tm_app (rho y) (rho x) ✓. I think it holds generally ✓ since opening replaces bvars and rho replaces fvars, disjoint.

   Wait, subtle: if u has free bvars, opening with u inserts them; on the RHS, the same u (rho-inst) is inserted ✓.
   
3. Combining: inst sigma rho (open_tm t u) = open_tm (inst sigma rho t) (inst sigma rho u). In our use u = tm_fvar x → inst = rho x ✓. So:
   `inst_open_tm : forall sigma rho t u, inst sigma rho (open_tm t u) = open_tm (inst sigma rho t) (inst sigma rho u)`.
   
4. `tm_ty_inst_open_tm_ty`: tm_ty_inst s (open_tm_ty t U) = open_tm_ty (tm_ty_inst s t) (ty_inst s U)? Let's check: open_tm_ty replaces the outermost free type bvar of t with U; tm_ty_inst replaces fvars by s's types. On the RHS, instantiate first then open with ty_inst s U. Should commute ✓. But careful: open_tm_ty_rec's index handling: tm_ty_inst doesn't change bvar structure ✓, and annotations: LHS annotations are open_ty_rec k U T then ty_inst s; RHS ty_inst s T then open_ty_rec k (ty_inst s U). We need commutativity of ty_inst and open_ty_rec: `ty_inst s (open_ty_rec k U T) = open_ty_rec k (ty_inst s U) (ty_inst s T)` ✓ (both replace fvars by s and the level-k bvar by U/s U). Should be provable by induction on T (with generalized k) ✓.
   
5. `tm_rho_inst_open_tm_ty`: tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm_ty t U) = open_tm_ty (tm_rho_inst rho t) U ✓ (they act on disjoint variables: open_tm_ty replaces type bvars and modifies annotations; rho-inst replaces term fvars and leaves annotations alone) ✓.
6. Combining: inst sigma rho (open_tm_ty t U) = open_tm_ty (inst sigma rho t) (ty_inst (sty sigma) U) ✓.

```
Theorem polymorphic_identity_theorem_for_free : forall t,
  has_type [] empty t (Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar 0))) ->
  forall U v, wf_ty [] U -> value v -> has_type [] empty v U -> tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v.
Proof.
  intros t Ht U v HwfU Hv Hvhv.
  (* fundamental theorem *)
  pose proof (fundamental Ht sigma0 rho0 Hrho0 Henvok) as HC.
  unfold C in HC. destruct HC as [Hlc [v0 [Hmulti0 HV0]]].
  (* HV0 : V [] sigma0 (All (Arrow (BVar 0) (BVar 0))) v0 *)
  simpl in HV0. (* V's Ty_All case *)
  destruct HV0 as [Hv0val HV0'].
  (* apply with U, HwfU-lc, R := fun w => w = v *)
  pose (R := fun w : tm => w = v).
  assert (HR : forall w, R w -> value w) by (intros w ->; exact Hv).
  pose proof (HV0' U (wf_ty_lc HwfU) R HR) as HC1.
  unfold C in HC1. destruct HC1 as [Hlc1 [v1 [Hmulti1 HV1]]].
  simpl in HV1. destruct HV1 as [Hv1val HV1'].
  (* V at BVar 0 with [s] = R *)
  ...

Let me think about the exact definition of `C` and whether to inline. I'll define V with inlined C as discussed:

```coq
Record sem_ty := {
  sty_ty : ty;
  sty_lc : locally_closed_ty sty_ty;
  sty_rel : tm -> Prop;
  sty_rel_value : forall t, sty_rel t -> value t
}.

Definition default_sem_ty : sem_ty.
Proof.
  refine {| sty_ty := Ty_All (Ty_BVar 0);
            sty_rel := fun _ => False |}.
  - apply lc_ty_all, lc_ty_bvar. lia.
  - intros t H; destruct H.
Defined.

Definition nth_sem (E : list sem_ty) (i : nat) : sem_ty :=
  match nth_error E i with Some s => s | None => default_sem_ty end.

Definition mk_sem (U : ty) (Hlc : locally_closed_ty U) (R : tm -> Prop)
  (HR : forall t, R t -> value t) : sem_ty :=
  {| sty_ty := U; sty_lc := Hlc; sty_rel := R; sty_rel_value := HR |}.

Fixpoint V (E : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => sty_rel (nth_sem E i) t
  | Ty_FVar X => sty_rel (sigma X) t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value t /\
      forall u, V E sigma T1 u ->
        locally_closed_tm (tm_app t u) /\ exists v, tm_app t u -->* v /\ V E sigma T2 v
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      value t /\
      forall (U : ty) (Hwf : locally_closed_ty U) (R : tm -> Prop) (HR : forall t, R t -> value t),
        locally_closed_tm (tm_tapp t U) /\ exists v, tm_tapp t U -->* v /\ V (mk_sem U Hwf R HR :: E) sigma T1 v
  end.

Definition C (E : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed_tm t /\ exists v, t -->* v /\ V E sigma T v.
```

Note: for the Ty_All semantics I replaced wf_ty [] U with locally_closed_ty U. Good — that makes the semantics simpler and the final application uses wf_ty_lc.

Now, in the fundamental theorem's T_TAbs case, the sem_ty s = mk_sem U Hlc R HR where Hlc comes from the ∀ semantics ✓.

Section 1: list/nat helpers.

```coq
Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l')
  end.

Lemma max_list_ge : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof. induction l; intros; destruct H; subst; simpl; lia. Qed.

Definition fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).

Lemma fresh_ok : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Proof. intros l H. pose proof (max_list_ge l (fresh l) H). unfold fresh in H0. lia. Qed.
```

Good.

Lemma lc_ty_at_mono : forall T k k', k <= k' -> lc_ty_at k T -> lc_ty_at k' T.

Opening inversion:
```coq
Lemma lc_ty_open_inv : forall k U T,
  (forall m, lc_ty_at m U) ->
  lc_ty_at k (open_ty_rec k U T) ->
  lc_ty_at (S k) T.
Proof.
  induction T; intros k U HU H; simpl in H.
  - (* BVar *) destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst. apply lc_ty_bvar; lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. inversion H; subst. apply lc_ty_bvar; lia.
  - (* FVar *) apply lc_ty_fvar.
  - (* Arrow *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_ty_arrow; auto.
  - (* All *) apply lc_ty_all. apply IHT with (k := S k) (U := U). auto.
    inversion H; subst. assumption.
Qed.
```

If false: H : lc_ty_at k (Ty_BVar n) → n < k; goal n < S k ✓. Careful about the variable naming: T = Ty_BVar n. `induction T` names the nat n. Let me use `n` consistently.

Term opening inversion:
```coq
Lemma lc_tm_open_inv : forall K n u t,
  (forall m, lc_tm_at K m u) ->
  lc_tm_at K n (open_tm_rec n u t) ->
  lc_tm_at K (S n) t.
Proof.
  induction t; intros K n u HU H; simpl in H.
  - (* bvar *) destruct (Nat.eqb n n0) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst. apply lc_tm_bvar; lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_bvar; lia.
  - (* fvar *) apply lc_tm_fvar.
  - (* abs *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_abs; auto.
  - (* app *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_app; auto.
  - (* tabs *) apply lc_tm_tabs. apply IHt with (K := S K) (n := n). auto.
    inversion H; subst. assumption.
  - (* tapp *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_tapp; auto.
  - (* choice *) inversion H; subst. apply lc_tm_choice; auto.
Qed.
```
Careful with the variable naming in induction: for tm_abs T t1, the case has T and t1. The IH is IHt1. For tm_tabs t1, IHt1. Let me be explicit in naming.

wf_ty_lc:
```coq
Lemma wf_ty_lc : forall Delta T, wf_ty Delta T -> locally_closed_ty T.
Proof.
  intros Delta T H. induction H.
  - apply lc_ty_fvar.
  - apply lc_ty_arrow; assumption.
  - (* WF_All *) apply lc_ty_all.
    set (X := fresh (L ++ atoms?)). 
```
Hmm, the WF_All case: H : forall X, ~ In X L -> wf_ty (X :: Delta) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)); IHWf : forall X, ~ In X L -> locally_closed_ty (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) (since the induction hypothesis is under the forall and the premise). Then pick X := fresh L: Hfresh : ~ In (fresh L) L; IHWf (fresh L) Hfresh : lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T (fvar X)) = lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty_rec 0 (fvar X) T). Apply lc_ty_open_inv with k := 0: need (forall m, lc_ty_at m (fvar X)) ✓ → lc_ty_at 1 T ✓. Then lc_ty_all ✓.

Careful: `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` is `open_ty_rec 0 (Ty_FVar X) T`. Definitional ✓.

lc_ty_inst:
```coq
Lemma lc_ty_inst : forall T k, lc_ty_at k T ->
  forall s, (forall X, locally_closed_ty (s X)) -> lc_ty_at k (ty_inst s T).
Proof.
  induction T; intros k Hk s Hs; simpl.
  - apply lc_ty_bvar; inversion Hk; lia.
  - apply lc_ty_at_mono with (k := 0); auto; lia.
  - apply lc_ty_arrow; inversion Hk; subst; auto.
  - apply lc_ty_all; inversion Hk; subst; auto.
Qed.
```

lc_ty_inst_at0: wf_ty Delta T -> locally_closed_ty (ty_inst s T) given all s lc. From wf_ty_lc + lc_ty_inst ✓.

has_type_lc:
```coq
Lemma has_type_lc : forall Delta Gamma t T, has_type Delta Gamma t T -> locally_closed_tm t.
Proof.
  intros Delta Gamma t T H; induction H.
  - apply lc_tm_fvar.  (* T_Var: t = tm_fvar x *)
  - (* T_Abs *) apply lc_tm_abs.
    + apply wf_ty_lc; assumption.
    + set (x := fresh (L ++ atoms_tm t2)).
      assert (Hx : ~ In x L) by (apply fresh_ok ... ) hmm.

The induction on has_type: the T_Abs constructor has a premise `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type Delta (x|->T1;Gamma) (open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x)) T2`. When we do `induction H`, the IH for this premise is `forall x, ~ In x L -> locally_closed_tm (open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x))`? Actually, the induction hypothesis generated by Coq for a premise of the form `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...` under `induction H` (on the derivation) — Coq's induction on the inductive predicate with a universally quantified premise: the IH will be for the premise as a whole? Hmm, actually the induction principle for has_type will quantify over the premise; the IH corresponds to the recursive occurrence under the forall. Let me think: the generated induction principle has, in the T_Abs case, the hypothesis `forall x, ~ In x L -> locally_closed_tm (open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x))` as the IH (it replaces has_type with the motive inside the forall). Yes, Coq's induction on H should give that.

lc_ty_open_fwd:
```coq
Lemma lc_ty_open_fwd : forall T k U,
  lc_ty_at (S k) T -> (forall m, lc_ty_at m U) -> lc_ty_at k (open_ty_rec k U T).
Proof.
  induction T; intros k U HT HU; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst; auto.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. inversion HT; subst. apply lc_ty_bvar; lia.
  - apply lc_ty_fvar.
  - apply lc_ty_arrow; inversion HT; subst; auto.
  - apply lc_ty_all. apply IHT with (k := S k) (U := U). inversion HT; subst; assumption. auto.
Qed.
```

Now, hmm, in the `has_type_lc_ty` T_TApp case: HT from IH1 : locally_closed_ty (Ty_All T) = lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_All T) → inversion gives lc_ty_at 1 T ✓. Then lc_ty_open_fwd with k=0: lc_ty_at (S 0) T ✓ and forall m, lc_ty_at m U ✓ (from wf_ty_lc Hwf via mono) → lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty_rec 0 U T) ✓.

Now Section D: instantiation definitions & commutation.

```coq
Definition sty (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) : atom -> ty := fun X => sty_ty (sigma X).

Fixpoint ty_inst (s : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => s X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_inst s T1) (ty_inst s T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_inst s T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_inst (s : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T b => tm_abs (ty_inst s T) (tm_ty_inst s b)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_ty_inst s t1) (tm_ty_inst s t2)
  | tm_tabs b => tm_tabs (tm_ty_inst s b)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_ty_inst s t1) (ty_inst s T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_ty_inst s t1) (tm_ty_inst s t2)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_rho_inst (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => rho x
  | tm_abs T b => tm_abs T (tm_rho_inst rho b)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_rho_inst rho t1) (tm_rho_inst rho t2)
  | tm_tabs b => tm_tabs (tm_rho_inst rho b)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_rho_inst rho t1) T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_rho_inst rho t1) (tm_rho_inst rho t2)
  end.

Definition inst (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  tm_rho_inst rho (tm_ty_inst (sty sigma) t).
```

Commutation lemmas:
```coq
Lemma ty_inst_open_ty : forall s k U T,
  ty_inst s (open_ty_rec k U T) = open_ty_rec k (ty_inst s U) (ty_inst s T).
Proof. induction T; intros; simpl; auto. 
  - (* BVar *) destruct (Nat.eqb k n); auto.
  - congruence.
Qed.
```
Wait, for Ty_BVar: open_ty_rec k U (BVar n) = if eqb k n then U else BVar n; ty_inst s of that = if eqb then ty_inst s U else BVar n; RHS: open_ty_rec k (ty_inst s U) (ty_inst s (BVar n)) = open_ty_rec k (ty_inst s U) (BVar n) = same ✓. destruct eqb; auto.
For Arrow/All: congruence or f_equal.

```coq

Proof. induction T; simpl; congruence. Qed.
```
Hmm, `ty_inst (fun X => Ty_FVar X) (Ty_FVar X) = Ty_FVar X` by beta; simpl should reduce ✓.

```coq

  tm_ty_inst s (open_tm_rec k u t) = open_tm_rec k (tm_ty_inst s u) (tm_ty_inst s t).
Proof. induction t; intros; simpl; auto; congruence. ... Qed.
```
For tm_bvar: open_tm_rec k u (tm_bvar n) = if eqb k n then u else bvar n; tm_ty_inst of that = if ... then tm_ty_inst s u else bvar n; RHS: open_tm_rec k (tm_ty_inst s u) (tm_ty_inst s (bvar n)) = if eqb k n then tm_ty_inst s u else bvar n ✓.

```coq
Lemma tm_rho_inst_open_tm : forall rho t u k,
  tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm_rec k u t) = open_tm_rec k (tm_rho_inst rho u) (tm_rho_inst rho t).
```
Same structure ✓.

```coq
Lemma tm_ty_inst_open_tm_ty : forall s t U k,
  tm_ty_inst s (open_tm_ty_rec k U t) = open_tm_ty_rec k (ty_inst s U) (tm_ty_inst s t).
Proof. induction t; intros; simpl; auto; 
  - (* abs *) rewrite IHt; rewrite ty_inst_open_ty; auto.
  - (* tabs *) rewrite IHt; auto.
  - (* tapp *) rewrite IHt; rewrite ty_inst_open_ty; auto.
  ...
```
Note open_tm_ty_rec under tm_tabs increments k: tm_ty_inst s (open_tm_ty_rec (S k) U t1) = open_tm_ty_rec (S k) (ty_inst s U) (tm_ty_inst s t1) ✓ by IH with k := S k.

```coq
Lemma tm_rho_inst_open_tm_ty : forall rho t U k,
  tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm_ty_rec k U t) = open_tm_ty_rec k U (tm_rho_inst rho t).
```
Induction: annotations unchanged by rho-inst; under abs: tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm_ty_rec k U (tm_abs T b)) = tm_rho_inst rho (tm_abs (open_ty_rec k U T) (open_tm_ty_rec k U b)) = tm_abs (open_ty_rec k U T) (tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm_ty_rec k U b)); RHS = open_tm_ty_rec k U (tm_abs T (tm_rho_inst rho b)) = tm_abs (open_ty_rec k U T) (open_tm_ty_rec k U (tm_rho_inst rho b)) ✓ by IH.

Combined:
```coq
Lemma inst_open_tm : forall sigma rho t u,
  inst sigma rho (open_tm t u) = open_tm (inst sigma rho t) (inst sigma rho u).
Proof. intros; unfold inst, open_tm; rewrite tm_ty_inst_open_tm, tm_rho_inst_open_tm; auto. Qed.

Lemma inst_open_tm_ty : forall sigma rho t U,
  inst sigma rho (open_tm_ty t U) = open_tm_ty (inst sigma rho t) (ty_inst (sty sigma) U).
Proof. intros; unfold inst, open_tm_ty; rewrite tm_ty_inst_open_tm_ty, tm_rho_inst_open_tm_ty; auto. Qed.
```
Careful with the exact forms: inst sigma rho (open_tm t u) = tm_rho_inst rho (tm_ty_inst sty (open_tm_rec 0 u t)); rewrite tm_ty_inst_open_tm: = tm_rho_inst rho (open_tm_rec 0 (tm_ty_inst sty u) (tm_ty_inst sty t)); rewrite tm_rho_inst_open_tm: = open_tm_rec 0 (tm_rho_inst rho (tm_ty_inst sty u)) (tm_rho_inst rho (tm_ty_inst sty t)) = open_tm (inst ... u) (inst ... t) ✓.

Extensionality lemmas:
```coq
Fixpoint atoms_ty (T : ty) : list atom := ...
Fixpoint atoms_tm (t : tm) : list atom := ...
Definition atoms_context (Gamma : context) : list atom := flat_map (fun p => atoms_ty (snd p)) Gamma.

Lemma ty_inst_ext : forall s s' T, (forall X, In X (atoms_ty T) -> s X = s' X) -> ty_inst s T = ty_inst s' T.
Lemma tm_ty_inst_ext : forall s s' t, (forall X, In X (atoms_tm t) -> s X = s' X) -> tm_ty_inst s t = tm_ty_inst s' t.
Lemma tm_rho_inst_ext : forall rho rho' t, (forall x, In x (atoms_tm t) -> rho x = rho' x) -> tm_rho_inst rho t = tm_rho_inst rho' t.
```
Note atoms_tm includes type atoms in annotations and term fvars. Good.

Also we need: if X ∉ atoms_ty T then X is not a free fvar of T and not... Actually the ext lemmas require agreement on atoms; if X ∉ atoms_ty T, then sigma_X (which differs from sigma possibly only at X) agrees with sigma on atoms_ty T ✓. Define sigma_upd:
```coq
Definition upd_sem (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (X : atom) (s : sem_ty) : atom -> sem_ty :=
  fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then s else sigma Y.

Lemma upd_sem_agree : forall sigma X s Y, Y <> X -> upd_sem sigma X s Y = sigma Y.
Lemma upd_sem_X : forall sigma X s, upd_sem sigma X s X = s.
```
Similarly upd_rho:
```coq
Definition upd_rho (rho : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else rho y.
```

Ext agreement for upd: if X ∉ atoms_ty T then forall Y, In Y (atoms_ty T) -> upd_sem sigma X s Y = sigma Y ✓ (Y ≠ X since X ∉ atoms).

Now the alpha/fresh lemmas: we need `~ In X (atoms_ty T)` from X := fresh (L ++ atoms_ty T ++ ...) ✓ via fresh_ok and list In_app.

Let me also think about what's needed in the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case for the fresh x: x := fresh (L ++ atoms_tm t2). Then x ∉ atoms_tm t2 ✓ and x ∉ L ✓.

```coq
Lemma V_value : forall E sigma T t, V E sigma T t -> value t.
Proof. induction T; simpl; intros; try (destruct H; auto); apply sty_rel_value; auto. Qed.
```

But `induction T` on a ty with V containing T... V is a Fixpoint; `induction T` should work (T is a ty). The IH is about subterms. Actually we don't even need the IH. Let me write:
```coq
Lemma V_value : forall E sigma T t, V E sigma T t -> value t.
Proof.
  intros E sigma T. induction T; simpl; intros t H.
  - apply sty_rel_value; exact H.
  - apply sty_rel_value; exact H.
  - destruct H as [Hv _]; exact Hv.
  - destruct H as [Hv _]; exact Hv.
Qed.
```
Note: `induction T` before introducing t and H, giving IHT1, IHT2 but unused. Fine.

```coq
Lemma C_value : forall E sigma T v, V E sigma T v -> C E sigma T v.
Proof. intros; unfold C; split; [apply value_lc, V_value; auto | exists v; split; [apply multi_refl | auto]]. Qed.

Lemma C_multi : forall E sigma T t t', t -->* t' -> C E sigma T t' -> locally_closed_tm t -> C E sigma T t.
Proof. intros; unfold C in *; destruct H0 as [Hlc [v [Hv HV]]]; split; auto; exists v; split; auto. eapply multi_trans; eauto. Qed.

Lemma C_step : forall E sigma T t t', step t t' -> C E sigma T t' -> locally_closed_tm t -> C E sigma T t.
Proof. intros; eapply C_multi; [apply multi_step with t'; auto| auto | auto]. Qed.
```

Lemma multi_trans : forall a b c, a -->* b -> b -->* c -> a -->* c.

Now Section F: V_prefix, V_sigma_agree, V_open_general.

V_sigma_agree:
```coq
Lemma ty_inst_ext ... (already).
Lemma V_sigma_agree : forall T sigma sigma',
  (forall X, In X (atoms_ty T) -> sigma X = sigma' X) ->
  forall E t, V E sigma T t <-> V E sigma' T t.
Proof.
  induction T; simpl; intros sigma sigma' Hag E t; auto.
  - (* Arrow *) split; intros [Hv H]; split; auto; intros u Hu; 
    specialize (H u); destruct H as [Hlc [v [Hm Hv]]]; split; auto; exists v; split; auto;
    apply (proj1 (IHT2 sigma sigma' _ E v) Hv) — hmm need to manage directions.
```
I'll write it carefully with two directions. The agreement hypothesis for subterms: atoms_ty T1 ⊆ atoms_ty (Arrow T1 T2) → from Hag; use `Hag Y (in_app_iff...)`. Simpler: use the ext lemma approach: define `sigma_agree_on (l : list atom) sigma sigma'`. Or prove via a helper: if Hag : forall X, In X (atoms_ty T) -> sigma X = sigma' X, then for T1, `fun Y HY => Hag Y (atoms_ty_subset ...)`. Need subset lemmas for atoms_ty subterms: e.g., `atoms_ty T1 ⊆ atoms_ty (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)`. Prove with `in_app_iff` and `auto`.

Maybe simpler: prove a version with a *global* pointwise equality for fvars that occur, and in the proof use `intros Y HY; apply Hag; simpl; auto` — where `simpl` on the goal `In Y (atoms_ty (Ty_Arrow T1 T2))` yields `In Y (atoms_ty T1 ++ atoms_ty T2)`, then `apply in_app_iff; auto`. This works with `auto` if `in_app_iff` is a hint? Not necessarily. Let me write explicit: `apply in_app_iff; left; auto` etc. But the direction depends on the subterm. Using `auto with datatypes`? Hmm, `in_app_iff` might be in the default hint database. In Rocq's Stdlib, `in_app_iff : forall (A : Type) (l l' : list A) (a : A), a ∈ l ++ l' <-> a ∈ l \/ a ∈ l'` and it's usually declared as a hint (`Hint Resolve in_app_iff`?). I'll test.

Let me define a helper:
```coq
Lemma atoms_ty_app_l : forall A B x, In x A -> In x (A ++ B).
Lemma atoms_ty_app_r ...
```
Actually generic list lemmas: `in_app_iff` and `in_or_app`. Let me use `apply in_app_iff; auto` — should be fine.

The V_sigma_agree proof:
- BVar: V doesn't use sigma ✓.
- FVar X: atoms_ty (fvar X) = [X] → Hag X (or_introl eq_refl) gives sigma X = sigma' X → rewrite ✓.
- Arrow: IHT1, IHT2 with agreement restricted; the C part: C E sigma T2 y = lc y ∧ exists v, y -->* v ∧ V E sigma T2 v; use IHT2 on the V part ✓.
- All: the forall quantifies over sem_ty and calls V with E' := mk_sem... :: E; IHT with E := E' ✓ (IH is forall E t).

OK. Since the IH is `forall E t, V E sigma T1 t <-> ...` if we set up the induction with sigma/sigma' introduced before E,t? The statement: forall T sigma sigma', agree -> forall E t, ... Then `induction T; intros sigma sigma' Hag E t`. The IH would be `forall sigma sigma', agree(T1) -> forall E t, V E sigma T1 t <-> ...` ✓.

V_prefix:
```coq
Lemma V_prefix : forall T K, lc_ty_at K T ->
  forall (pre : list sem_ty), length pre = K ->
  forall A B sigma t, V (pre ++ A) sigma T t <-> V (pre ++ B) sigma T t.
Proof.
  induction T; intros K HK pre Hlen A B sigma t; simpl.
  - (* BVar *) inversion HK; subst. 
    rewrite !nth_sem_app_fix; auto.
```
Hmm, the bvar lookup: V (pre ++ A) sigma (BVar n) t = sty_rel (nth_sem (pre ++ A) n) t. Need nth_sem (pre ++ A) n = nth_sem (pre ++ B) n, knowing n < K = length pre. Prove a lemma:
`Lemma nth_sem_prefix : forall pre A B n, n < length pre -> nth_sem (pre ++ A) n = nth_sem (pre ++ B) n.`
Proof: rewrite nth_sem as match nth_error...; use nth_error_app1 (n < length pre) for both ✓.

- FVar ✓ (simpl; auto).
- Arrow: split; intros [Hv H]; split; auto; intros u Hu; specialize H with u; destruct H as [Hlc [v [Hm HV]]]; split; auto; exists v; split; auto;

(* ===== Helper: list max / freshness ===== *)
Fixpoint lmax (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (lmax l')
  end.

Lemma lmax_spec : forall l x, In x l -> x <= lmax l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y l IH]; intros x Hx.
  - destruct Hx.
  - simpl in Hx. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (m := lmax l); [apply IH; auto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Definition fresh_atom (l : list atom) : atom := S (lmax l).

Lemma fresh_atom_spec : forall l, ~ In (fresh_atom l) l.
Proof.
  intros l H. unfold fresh_atom in H. apply lmax_spec in H. lia.
Qed.
```

Then atoms:
```coq
Fixpoint atoms_ty (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => atoms_ty T1 ++ atoms_ty T2
  | Ty_All T1 => atoms_ty T1
  end.

Fixpoint atoms_tm (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs T b => atoms_ty T ++ atoms_tm b
  | tm_app t1 t2 => atoms_tm t1 ++ atoms_tm t2
  | tm_tabs b => atoms_tm b
  | tm_tapp b T => atoms_tm b ++ atoms_ty T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => atoms_tm t1 ++ atoms_tm t2
  end.

Definition atoms_context (Gamma : context) : list atom :=
  flat_map (fun p => atoms_ty (snd p)) Gamma.
```

```coq
Lemma V_open_general : forall (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) k (T V0 : ty) (s : sem_ty),
  lc_ty_at (S k) T ->
  (forall (E' : list sem_ty) t, V E' sigma V0 t <-> sty_rel s t) ->
  forall (E : list sem_ty), length E = k ->
  forall t, V E sigma (open_ty_rec k V0 T) t <-> V (E ++ [s]) sigma T t.
```

Additional lemmas for `nth_sem`:
```coq
Lemma nth_sem_app_l : forall E E' i, i < length E -> nth_sem (E ++ E') i = nth_sem E i.
Proof.
  intros E E' i Hi. unfold nth_sem.
  rewrite nth_error_app1; auto.
Qed.
Lemma nth_sem_app_len : forall E s, nth_sem (E ++ [s]) (length E) = s.
Proof.
  intros E s. unfold nth_sem.
  rewrite nth_error_app2 by lia.
  replace (length E - length E) with 0 by lia. simpl. auto.
Qed.
Lemma nth_sem_cons_0 : forall s E, nth_sem (s :: E) 0 = s.

Proof. intros; reflexivity. Qed.

```coq
(* ------------------------------------------------------------------ *)
(* Basic list helpers                                                   *)
(* ------------------------------------------------------------------ *)

Fixpoint lmax (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (lmax l')
  end.

Lemma lmax_spec : forall l x, In x l -> x <= lmax l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y l IH]; intros x Hx.
  - destruct Hx.
  - simpl in Hx. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; eauto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Definition fresh_atom (l : list atom) : atom := S (lmax l).

Lemma fresh_atom_spec : forall l, ~ In (fresh_atom l) l.
Proof.
  intros l H. unfold fresh_atom in H. apply lmax_spec in H. lia.
Qed.
```

Let me rewrite these three proofs with explicit as-patterns.

wf_ty constructors:
```
| WF_Var : forall Delta X, In X Delta -> wf_ty Delta (Ty_FVar X)
| WF_Arrow : forall Delta T1 T2, wf_ty Delta T1 -> wf_ty Delta T2 -> wf_ty Delta (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)
| WF_All : forall (L : list atom) Delta T, (forall X, ~ In X L -> wf_ty (X :: Delta) (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X))) -> wf_ty Delta (Ty_All T)
```
So:
`induction H as [Delta X Hin | Delta T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | L Delta T Hbody IHb]`.

For WF_All: IHb : forall X, ~ In X L -> locally_closed_ty (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)).

Proof:
```
- apply lc_ty_all.
  set (X := fresh_atom L).
  assert (HX : ~ In X L) by (unfold X; apply fresh_atom_spec).
  specialize (IHb X HX).
  (* IHb : locally_closed_ty (open_ty T (fvar X)) *)
  eapply lc_ty_open_inv with (k := 0) (U := Ty_FVar X); [intros; apply lc_ty_fvar | ].
  simpl. exact IHb.  (* open_ty T (fvar X) unfolds to open_ty_rec 0 (fvar X) T *)
```
Careful: locally_closed_ty (open_ty T (fvar X)) is lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T ...), and open_ty T U = open_ty_rec 0 U T ✓ definitionally.

has_type constructors and their argument names:

T_Var Delta Gamma x T Hlook Hwf

T_Choice Delta Gamma t1 t2 T H1 H2
```
Patterns: `induction H as [Delta Gamma x T Hlook Hwf | L Delta Gamma T1 t2 T2 Hwf Hbody IHb | Delta Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | L Delta Gamma t T Hbody IHb | Delta Gamma t T U H1 IH1 Hwf | Delta Gamma t1 t2 T H1 IH1 H2 IH2]`.

```coq
Lemma open_ty_rec_lc : forall T k V, lc_ty_at 0 T -> open_ty_rec k V T = T.

  - auto.

```coq
Lemma open_ty_rec_lc_gen : forall T n k V, lc_ty_at n T -> open_ty_rec (n + k) V T = T.
Proof.

Excellent! Part 4 compiles now (module needs closing = everything compiled). Let me now write Part 5: the V and C definitions and their basic lemmas.

Definitions:
```coq
Fixpoint V (E : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => sty_rel (nth_sem E i) t
  | Ty_FVar X => sty_rel (sigma X) t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value t /\
      forall u, V E sigma T1 u ->
        locally_closed_tm (tm_app t u) /\ (exists v, tm_app t u -->* v /\ V E sigma T2 v)
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      value t /\
      forall (U : ty) (Hlc : locally_closed_ty U) (R : tm -> Prop) (HR : forall w, R w -> value w),
        locally_closed_tm (tm_tapp t U) /\ (exists v, tm_tapp t U -->* v /\ V (mk_sem U Hlc R HR :: E) sigma T1 v)
  end.

Definition C (E : list sem_ty) (sigma : atom -> sem_ty) (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed_tm t /\ exists v, t -->* v /\ V E sigma T v.
```

Note: V's arrow case should ideally be exactly C E sigma T2 (tm_app t u), which unfolds to the same thing ✓ definitionally. I could write `C` there only if C is defined before V — it isn't. But since the definitions are definitionally equal, after defining C, `C E sigma T2 (tm_app t u)` unfolds to the same. Good.

Lemma V_value : forall E sigma T t, V E sigma T t -> value t.
